        <script>
          function getDfd() {let yFn,nFn;const p=new Promise(function(y, n){yFn=y;nFn=n;});p.resolve=yFn;p.reject=nFn;return p;}
          window.lazyloader = getDfd();
          window.tracking = getDfd();
          window.impressionTracking = getDfd();
          window.ingraphTracking = getDfd();
          window.appDetection = getDfd();
          window.pemTracking = getDfd();
          window.appRedirectCompleted = getDfd();
        </script>

  <meta name="description" content="You can cut Claude Code's tool calls by 94% with one command. 

When Claude Code explores a repo, it spawns agents to scan files. 

Each scan burns tokens on every tool call.
 
CodeGraph is an open-source MCP server that fixes this. 

It pre-indexes your codebase into a local knowledge graph upfront.
 
Instead of scanning, the agent queries the graph directly. 

One call returns entry points, related symbols, and code snippets.
 
Tested across six real-world projects, the results showed:
 
> 92% fewer tool calls average
> 71% faster exploration overall
> 94% drop on a TypeScript repo
> 96% drop on a Java codebase
 
Tree-sitter parses source into syntax trees. 

SQLite stores symbols, callers, and edges with full-text search.
 
A file watcher keeps everything fresh as you code. 

It supports 19+ languages and runs fully on your machine.
 
Setup is one npx command. No API keys are required. 

Link in comments.
↓
Check out AlphaSignal.ai to get a daily summary of top models, repos, and papers in AI. Read by 280,000+ devs. | 58 comments on LinkedIn">

        {"@context":"http://schema.org","@type":"SocialMediaPosting","@id":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/eric-vyacheslav-156273169_you-can-cut-claude-codes-tool-calls-by-94-activity-7458184457297092608-riev","datePublished":"2026-05-07T16:02:20.957Z","headline":"You can cut Claude Code's tool calls by 94% with one command.","comment":[{"@type":"Comment","datePublished":"2026-05-07T16:02:21.150Z","text":"Repo: https://github.com/colbymchenry/codegraph","author":{"@type":"Person","name":"Eric Vyacheslav"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":18}},{"@type":"Comment","datePublished":"2026-05-07T16:58:21.357Z","text":"Having Claude create reference docs of your code base is far more efficient than calling an MCP. Skills that define keeping the reference docs updated after commits is also a handy little tip. When I need a new session to understand part of my code base, I point to the reference doc, no need to scan code or make an MCP call","author":{"@type":"Person","name":"David Moskowitz"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":34}},{"@type":"Comment","datePublished":"2026-05-25T12:58:17.045Z","text":"Reference docs are separated by function. When you work on your app, even several thousand files, you're not editing everything, just a small section of the code base. Therefore, only a single reference doc needs to be read, not the entire library. If you're running into this issue, your implementation plan needs to create more discreet phases for the agent. You will get higher quality agent output with more phases, which requires less input tokens when starting each session, and saves your context window for the output.","author":{"@type":"Person","name":"David Moskowitz"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":0}},{"@type":"Comment","datePublished":"2026-05-10T17:07:15.083Z","text":"I put codegraph on steroids here -\u003E https://tokensave.devFaster, more tools, more languages, more features, no runtime requirements.\n","author":{"@type":"Person","name":"Enzo Lombardi"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":3}},{"@type":"Comment","datePublished":"2026-05-08T15:08:47.797Z","text":"Since the inception of Linux, directory structure is a folder in a folder in a folder… That’s a tree! A root, with children, with children, down to leafs! Knowing this, one can orchestrate their agent to traverse the folders depth first or breadth first; and with proper naming conventions of folders and files that is all the context an LLM should need. Optional: some extra context in markdown files along the traversal path. Scalable, mutable, maintainable, local. \n\nhttps://arxiv.org/html/2603.16021v2","author":{"@type":"Person","name":"Dean Macabbi Shalem"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":1}},{"@type":"Comment","datePublished":"2026-05-08T17:49:00.037Z","text":"The teams that use CodeGraph will have faster, cheaper agent exploration. The ones that rely on scanning will burn tokens and time. The difference is not the model. It's the architecture around it. The MCP server pre-indexes. The agent queries directly. The file watcher keeps it fresh. The link is in comments. The command is one npx. The result is 94% fewer tool calls.","author":{"@type":"Person","name":"Awais Naeem"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":0}},{"@type":"Comment","datePublished":"2026-05-12T08:17:22.897Z","text":" Nice, built a similar one (mcp-repo-graph / glia). Same tree-sitter base, but stored as a zero-copy mmap'd binary instead of SQLite, with cross-stack edges (frontend  fetch ↔ backend route handlers) and PPR activation on top of lookup. The 94% number is really \"vs unguided grep\" i guess any pre-indexed MCP lands near there.","author":{"@type":"Person","name":"James Chahwan"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":0}},{"@type":"Comment","datePublished":"2026-05-07T17:41:20.420Z","text":"That’s a clever benchmark angle. Tool-call count is a sneaky proxy for how much unnecessary thrashing an agent is doing, and 94% is big enough that it changes whether these workflows feel usable in practice. Curious how much of the gain came from better planning vs tighter tool/result formatting vs prompt structure.","author":{"@type":"Person","name":"Chat Data"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":0}},{"@type":"Comment","datePublished":"2026-05-13T14:37:33.523Z","text":"I rely on https://pi.dev/packages/pi-hashline-readmap :-)\n\nBenefits: almost zero context cost. MCP server crap hogs 10Ks of context by itself alone.","author":{"@type":"Person","name":"Jarkko Sakkinen"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":0}},{"@type":"Comment","datePublished":"2026-05-08T06:27:47.456Z","text":"Eric, it’s interesting that the industry seems to be solving similar problems across the LLM tooling landscape.\n\nI started building cxpak (https://github.com/Barnett-Studios/cxpak) to solve this exact issue.   <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        " dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">You can cut Claude Code's tool calls by 94% with one command. 

When Claude Code explores a repo, it spawns agents to scan files. 

Each scan burns tokens on every tool call.
 
CodeGraph is an open-source MCP server that fixes this. 

It pre-indexes your codebase into a local knowledge graph upfront.
 
Instead of scanning, the agent queries the graph directly. 

One call returns entry points, related symbols, and code snippets.
 
Tested across six real-world projects, the results showed:
 
&gt; 92% fewer tool calls average
&gt; 71% faster exploration overall
&gt; 94% drop on a TypeScript repo
&gt; 96% drop on a Java codebase
 
Tree-sitter parses source into syntax trees. 

SQLite stores symbols, callers, and edges with full-text search.
 
A file watcher keeps everything fresh as you code. 

It supports 19+ languages and runs fully on your machine.
 
Setup is one npx command. No API keys are required. 

Link in comments.
↓
Check out <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/redir/redirect?url=http%3A%2F%2FAlphaSignal%2Eai&amp;urlhash=DMgk&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>AlphaSignal.ai</a> to get a daily summary of top models, repos, and papers in AI. Read by 280,000+ devs.</p>

         comment__text babybear:mt-0.5" dir="ltr">Having Claude create reference docs of your code base is far more efficient than calling an MCP.   <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
         comment__text babybear:mt-0.5" dir="ltr">Reference docs are separated by function. When you work on your app, even several thousand files, you're not editing everything, just a small section of the code base. Therefore, only a single reference doc needs to be read, not the entire library. If you're running into this issue, your implementation plan needs to create more discreet phases for the agent. You will get higher quality agent output with more phases, which requires less input tokens when starting each session, and saves your context window for the output.</p>
<!---->  </div>

            
<!---->      
<!---->          </div>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
         comment__text babybear:mt-0.5" dir="ltr">Since the inception of Linux, directory structure is a folder in a folder in a folder… That’s a tree! A root, with children, with children, down to leafs! Knowing this, one can orchestrate their agent to traverse the folders depth first or breadth first; and with proper naming conventions of folders and files that is all the context an LLM should need. Optional: some extra context in markdown files along the traversal path. Scalable, mutable, maintainable, local. 

<a class="link" href="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.16021v2?trk=public_post_comment-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post_comment-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://arxiv.org/html/2603.16021v2</a></p>
<!---->  </div>

        </ul>

<!---->    </div>
  

      </div>
  
      
            </div>
            
  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
         comment__text babybear:mt-0.5" dir="ltr">The teams that use CodeGraph will have faster, cheaper agent exploration. The ones that rely on scanning will burn tokens and time. The difference is not the model. It's the architecture around it. The MCP server pre-indexes. The agent queries directly. The file watcher keeps it fresh. The link is in comments. The command is one npx. The result is 94% fewer tool calls.</p>
<!---->  </div>

    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
         comment__text babybear:mt-0.5" dir="ltr"> Nice, built a similar one (mcp-repo-graph / glia). Same tree-sitter base, but stored as a zero-copy mmap'd binary instead of SQLite, with cross-stack edges (frontend  fetch ↔ backend route handlers) and PPR activation on top of lookup. The 94% number is really "vs unguided grep" i guess any pre-indexed MCP lands near there.</p>

         comment__text babybear:mt-0.5" dir="ltr">That’s a clever benchmark angle.   <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
         comment__text babybear:mt-0.5" dir="ltr"><a class="link" href="https://il.linkedin.com/in/eric-vyacheslav-156273169?trk=public_post_comment-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post_comment-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Eric</a>, it’s interesting that the industry seems to be solving similar problems across the LLM tooling landscape.

I started building cxpak (<a class="link" href="https://github.com/Barnett-Studios/cxpak?trk=public_post_comment-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post_comment-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://github.com/Barnett-Studios/cxpak</a>) to solve this exact issue. Latest version is 2.1.0, so it’s somewhat more mature than the tool shared here — local server, MCP, Claude plugin, CLI, GUI, LSP. As many ways to use it as I could think of. And lots of features and tools under the hood, powered by Rust.

We are currently working on architectural changes to our company AI SDLC which revolve around this tool for one simple reason beyond tokens: it’s deterministic. And that’s one thing none of the harnesses do well.</p>
<!---->  </div>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        " dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">You can cut Claude Code's tool calls by 94% with one command. 

When Claude Code explores a repo, it spawns agents to scan files. 

Each scan burns tokens on every tool call.
 
CodeGraph is an open-source MCP server that fixes this. 

It pre-indexes your codebase into a local knowledge graph upfront.
 
Instead of scanning, the agent queries the graph directly. 

One call returns entry points, related symbols, and code snippets.
 
Tested across six real-world projects, the results showed:
 
&gt; 92% fewer tool calls average
&gt; 71% faster exploration overall
&gt; 94% drop on a TypeScript repo
&gt; 96% drop on a Java codebase
 
Tree-sitter parses source into syntax trees. 

SQLite stores symbols, callers, and edges with full-text search.
 
A file watcher keeps everything fresh as you code. 

It supports 19+ languages and runs fully on your machine.
 
Setup is one npx command. No API keys are required. 

Link in comments.
↓
Check out <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/redir/redirect?url=http%3A%2F%2FAlphaSignal%2Eai&amp;urlhash=DMgk&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>AlphaSignal.ai</a> to get a daily summary of top models, repos, and papers in AI. Read by 280,000+ devs.</p>
<!---->  </div>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        " dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">A developer just replaced your entire backend tooling stack with one open source tool. 

Every backend project starts the same way.
 
You wire up an API, a queue, a scheduler, and observability. 

That's four tools before any business logic ships.
 
An open-source engine called iii (pronounced as "three eye") collapses all of it into one runtime.
 
It runs on three primitives:
 
&gt; Function: a unit of work
&gt; Trigger: what makes it run
&gt; Worker: connects to the engine
 
Need a queue, sandbox, or agent in the stack?
 
One CLI command adds it. The new capability joins a live catalog instantly. 

Every other process is notified and can call it.
 
Agents use the same interface developers do.
 
The whole thing installs as a single binary. 

TypeScript, Python, and Rust are supported out of the box.
 
The repo just crossed 16K stars and is growing fast.
 
What happens when the backend has its React moment?

Link in comments.
↓
Check out <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/redir/redirect?url=http%3A%2F%2FAlphaSignal%2Eai&amp;urlhash=DMgk&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>AlphaSignal.ai</a> to get a daily summary of top models, repos, and papers in AI. Read by 300,000+ devs.</p>
<!---->  </div>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        " dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">I've been spending time recently doing structured deep dives into topics I want to genuinely understand — and implement simple toy models around those topics. So, over the weekend I spent a lot of time exploring web-sockets with Claude Code. 

The approach I followed:

1. Theory first. Before writing a single line of code, I spent time building a mental model — basic preview of how web sockets work, why they are so expensive, how statefulness affects every layer of the infrastructure, when to use / not-use WebSockets. Some real-world examples of when they are used and when they are not.

2. Design before implementation. I defined the scope and agreed on a test list before touching code. 

Scope: A WebSocket client and server where:
- The server echoes client messages
- The server can also push messages independently
- The client can close the connection cleanly

Test list (same 9 tests implemented in all three languages):
1. Server starts and listens on a port
2. Client connects to the server
3. On connection, client receives a welcome message from the server
4. Client sends a message and receives the echo correctly
5. Server sends an independent message, client receives it correctly
6. Client closes the connection cleanly (close code 1000)
7. Server handles multiple sequential messages correctly
8. Client handles multiple sequential messages correctly
9. Server handles multiple clients independently

3. Strict TDD. Kent Beck style — write the test list first, then one test at a time: red → green → refactor. No additional code before a failing test.

4. Three languages. Python (`websockets` + `pytest-asyncio`), Go (`gorilla/websocket`), and C# (`System.Net.WebSockets` + `xUnit`). I consider myself pretty proficient with python and use C# at work, but have been wanting to get a better understanding of Go for some time. 

Tell me how are you using Claude and other llms to learn and do things better?

P.S. - Repository link in comments, I typed in everything for the source code, and didn't use auto-edits at all. The article is generated by Claude, and edited by me. Any feedback is greatly appreciated!</p>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        " dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Worth knowing if you use Claude Code.

By default, it does text search. Fast text search (it uses ripgrep under the hood), but still text. Ask "where is processOrder defined?" and Claude searches your codebase, reads files, makes a judgment call. Works most of the time. Slow on big repos. Sometimes wrong.

The upgrade is LSP. Language Server Protocol. Same tech behind "Go to Definition" in VS Code. The thing that lets tools understand your code as code, not text.

With LSP on, Claude asks the language server directly. Exact file. Exact line. No reading files. No guessing.

The bigger win, honestly, is diagnostics. When Claude edits a function, the language server instantly flags every broken call site. Claude fixes them all in the same turn. Before any errors hit your screen.

The official Claude plugin library has LSP for 12 languages. Python, Go, Rust, Java, TypeScript, and more.

Two minutes to set up. Surprised it's not on by default.

<a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fclaudecode&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#ClaudeCode</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fai&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#AI</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fdevelopertools&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#DeveloperTools</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fprogramming&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#Programming</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Flsp&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#LSP</a></p>

    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        " dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">kreuzberg-txtai is live 🎉 Our TxtAI (by <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/company/neuml?trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>NeuML</a>) integration for <a class="link" href="https://de.linkedin.com/company/kreuzberg-dev?trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Kreuzberg</a> offers TxtAI developers a simple replacement for Textractor by replacing Apache Tika and its Java runtime with Kreuzberg’s Rust-based extraction engine. You don’t need to change your current pipeline interface to use it.

Add it to your TxtAI workflow, and you’ll get:
* Support for many formats like PDF, DOCX, PPTX, HTML, images, and more, all in one package
* No need for Java. Tika requires a Java Virtual Machine (JVM), which adds extra runtime overhead in every environment and container. Kreuzberg uses compiled Rust binaries instead, so you just need a pip install.
* You get consistent metadata for every document, including title, MIME type, page count, and source path. The format stays the same no matter the file type, so you don’t have to search through different output fields.
* Flexible OCR options let you choose from Tesseract, EasyOCR, or PaddleOCR, depending on your language and accuracy needs.
* You can pick your output format: plain text, Markdown, HTML, or Djot.

It’s designed to be minimal. This thin connector fits into your existing TxtAI pipeline without adding complexity.

This is the newest part of Kreuzberg’s ecosystem of AI framework integrations, joining <a class="link" href="https://uk.linkedin.com/company/surrealdb?trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>SurrealDB</a>, <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/company/langchain?trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>LangChain</a>, <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/company/llamaindex?trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>LlamaIndex</a>, <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/company/crewai-inc?trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>CrewAI</a>, <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/showcase/haystack-ai-framework/?trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Haystack AI Framework</a>, and Spring AI. It's MIT licensed and you’ll need Python 3.10+.

GitHub: <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/redir/redirect?url=https%3A%2F%2Flnkd%2Ein%2FdvdZVy63&amp;urlhash=EAug&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/dvdZVy63</a>

Let us know what you think and connect with the team on our Discord server: <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/redir/redirect?url=https%3A%2F%2Flnkd%2Ein%2FdjjurDdE&amp;urlhash=Hbm6&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/djjurDdE</a> </p>
<!---->  </div>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        " dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">I just shipped something I've been genuinely obsessed with 🤓  — I wrote a custom Claude Code Agent Skill to scaffold a full-stack NL→SQL agent from scratch using FastAPI and OpenAPI on Python 3.12, Supabase for PostgreSQL, Claude API handling the language side. You type a question in plain English, it gives you the SQL it generated, the actual results in a table, and an explanation you can read without knowing SQL. 

And no, this wasn't Vibe Coding but definitely Claude Code helped to speed the process. I rprepared a 6-phase development workflow: Understanding → Planning → Implementation → Testing → Verification → Documentation → Commit. Every phase intentional. The way it should be done.

The moment it all clicked together was something. User logs in, types "show me all orders from last month over $500", and seconds later they're looking at real data from PostgreSQL. Query history were saved per user, so nothing disappears between sessions.

The hardest part wasn't the AI. It was stitching auth , persistence, and the API layer together cleanly. But that's exactly why I'm glad I built it this way. 👏 </p>
<!---->  </div>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        " dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Your LLM endpoint returns a beautiful paragraph. Your frontend dev pings you:

&gt; "What field is the price in?"
&gt; "It's… in the sentence." 😬

That's the moment you reach for structured output.

Just published Post 6 of my Spring AI RAG series — Structured Output: Turning LLM Prose into Typed Java Records.

The whole API is one method call: swap .content() for .entity(<a class="link" href="http://FaqEntry.class?trk=public_post-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>FaqEntry.class</a>) and you get a typed Java record instead of a String. Same ChatClient, same prompt, same RAG. One line different.

And it's not magic: Spring AI generates a JSON schema from your record, appends it to your prompt, and parses the response with Jackson.

A few things I had to learn the hard way:
→ Use String for almost everything (enums and dates will bite you)
→ Keep records flat and short — long schemas = more LLM mistakes
→ Field names are part of the prompt: releaseDate beats dt
→ Always wrap with retry + validation. The LLM will eventually return invalid JSON.

Use it when the next consumer is code, not a human.

Read the full post:
<a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/redir/redirect?url=https%3A%2F%2Flnkd%2Ein%2FdxwJRJhU&amp;urlhash=MAjY&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/dxwJRJhU</a>

<a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fspringai&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#SpringAI</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Frag&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#RAG</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fjava&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#Java</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fspringboot&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#SpringBoot</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fllm&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#LLM</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Faiengineering&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#AIEngineering</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/signup/cold-join?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fstructuredoutput&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#StructuredOutput</a></p>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        " dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">10 weeks ago I started building FairSplit+ from scratch.

Today I'm calling it shipped.

Here's everything that went into it:

Week 1-2: Domain modeling. Users, groups, expenses, splits, settlements. The schema decisions you make here follow you forever.

Week 3-4: Core APIs. Auth with JWT, group management, expense creation with EQUAL, EXACT, and PERCENTAGE split types.

Week 5: Debt simplification. A greedy algorithm using PriorityQueues that reduces N debts down to N-1 transactions. This is the core feature that makes the math actually fair.

Week 6: AI expense parser. Type "split $90 dinner with Bob equally" and Claude parses the intent, extracts the amount and split type, and either auto-creates the expense or asks for clarification based on a confidence score.

Week 7: Split type modeling. Three ways to divide an expense, each with its own validation logic.

Week 8: Deployment. Docker, Railway, Postgres in production. Three migration failures, one DATABASE_URL format mismatch, and one missing V1 schema file later, it was live.

Week 9: React frontend. Login, register, dashboard, group detail, AI parse tab. Deployed to Vercel.

Week 10: Activity feed. A JSONB-backed event log that turns database rows into human-readable group history.

What I actually learned:

The domain model is the product. Get that wrong and everything else is harder.

Deployment is where you find out what you don't know. Anyone can write code that works locally.

AI features are UX decisions first. The confidence gate on the expense parser was more important than the parser itself.

Building in public keeps you honest. You can't hide behind "I'll clean it up later" when you're shipping every week.

The project is live. The code is open.

Live: <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/redir/redirect?url=https%3A%2F%2Flnkd%2Ein%2Fg4qy5ffy&amp;urlhash=1mD3&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/g4qy5ffy</a>
GitHub: <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/redir/redirect?url=https%3A%2F%2Flnkd%2Ein%2FgkrGzfDs&amp;urlhash=-pV4&amp;trk=public_post-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/gkrGzfDs</a>

Stack: Java 21 · Spring Boot 3 · PostgreSQL · Redis · React 18 · Tailwind · Docker · Railway · Vercel

If you followed along, thank you. It made the process better.

On to the next one.

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        " dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">𝗜 𝗷𝘂𝘀𝘁 𝘀𝗵𝗶𝗽𝗽𝗲𝗱 𝘀𝗼𝗺𝗲𝘁𝗵𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗜 𝗵𝗮𝘃𝗲 𝗯𝗲𝗲𝗻 𝘄𝗮𝗻𝘁𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝘁𝗼 𝗯𝘂𝗶𝗹𝗱 𝗳𝗼𝗿 𝗮 𝘄𝗵𝗶𝗹𝗲.

𝗔𝗢𝗟 𝗠𝗮𝗶𝗹 𝗠𝗖𝗣 𝗦𝗲𝗿𝘃𝗲𝗿 — an open source Python server that connects your AOL mailbox directly to AI assistants like Claude Desktop and VS Code GitHub Copilot Agent Mode.

Once configured, you can literally say:

"𝗦𝗵𝗼𝘄 𝗺𝗲 𝘂𝗻𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗱 𝗲𝗺𝗮𝗶𝗹𝘀 𝗳𝗿𝗼𝗺 𝗹𝗮𝘀𝘁 𝘄𝗲𝗲𝗸"
"𝗥𝗲𝗽𝗹𝘆 𝘁𝗼 𝗝𝗼𝗵𝗻'𝘀 𝗲𝗺𝗮𝗶𝗹 𝗮𝗻𝗱 𝘀𝗮𝘆 𝗜'𝗹𝗹 𝗯𝗲 𝘁𝗵𝗲𝗿𝗲 𝗮𝘁 𝟯𝗽𝗺"

𝗔𝗻𝗱 𝗶𝘁 𝗷𝘂𝘀𝘁 𝘄𝗼𝗿𝗸𝘀.

The server exposes 10 tools over the Model Context Protocol:
Read inbox · Search emails · Send and reply · Delete and move · Mark as read · List attachments

Built with Python, FastMCP, and standard library IMAP and SMTP. No third party email SDKs. Published to PyPI so anyone can run it with one command:

𝘂𝘃𝘅 𝗮𝗼𝗹-𝗺𝗰𝗽

Full source code, setup guide, and Claude Desktop and VS Code config instructions are on GitHub.

𝗚𝗶𝘁𝗛𝘂𝗯: 𝗴𝗶𝘁𝗵𝘂𝗯.𝗰𝗼𝗺/𝗸𝘂𝗯𝗲𝗴𝗿𝗶𝗻𝗱/𝗮𝗼𝗹-𝗺𝗰𝗽-𝘀𝗲𝗿𝘃𝗲𝗿
𝗣𝘆𝗣𝗜: 𝗽𝘆𝗽𝗶.𝗼𝗿𝗴/𝗽𝗿𝗼𝗷𝗲𝗰𝘁/𝗮𝗼𝗹-𝗺𝗰𝗽

If you use AOL Mail and want to manage it through AI, give it a try. Feedback and contributions are very welcome.

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        " dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Your CLI tool ships to 50,000 developers. The language choice determines whether they install it or abandon it at the first error.

Language selection for CLI tools is not about developer preference. It is about operational constraints under distribution.

If you choose a runtime language, you inherit the runtime's failure surface. <a class="link" href="http://Node.js?trk=public_post-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="public_post-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Node.js</a> CLIs fail when the user's Node version conflicts with your dependencies. Python CLIs fail when the user's environment has package conflicts. Ruby CLIs fail when gem versions don't align.

The failure mode is environmental mismatch. Your tool works in your environment. It breaks in theirs.

If you choose a compiled language, you trade runtime failures for build complexity. Rust CLIs ship as single binaries with zero external dependencies. Go CLIs ship as single binaries with predictable memory usage. C++ CLIs ship as single binaries that segfault on edge cases you didn't test.

The failure mode shifts from environmental to correctness.

Distribution weight matters more than development speed. A 50MB binary installs faster than a runtime + package manager + dependency resolution + environment setup. Users measure CLI tools by time-to-first-success, not lines of code written.

Your language choice is downstream of your error budget. If you can afford environmental debugging across user machines, runtime languages work. If you need predictable installation, compiled languages work.

The constraint is not performance. The constraint is failure modes under distribution.</p>
